Entanglement area law in interacting bosons from the Bose-Hubbard model to ϕ \phi 4 theory and beyond
Donghoon Kim 1, Tomotaka Kuwahara 1,2,3

Abstract
The entanglement area law is a universal principle that characterizes quantum many-body phases and underpins tensor network algorithms. Traditionally, its validity has been limited to systems with short-range interactions and bounded local energy. Achieving a complete generalization that removes both of these constraints has been a longstanding goal in quantum many-body theory, especially for interacting boson systems where unbounded energy presents intrinsic difficulties. In this work, we rigorously prove the area law for one-dimensional interacting boson systems with long-range interactions, covering broad models including the Bose-Hubbard and ϕ ​ 4 \phi 4 classes. Furthermore, we establish an efficiency guarantee for Matrix-Product-State approximations of the ground states, offering a practical route to numerical simulation. One of our main technical contributions is a general method for Hilbert space dimension reduction, whose applicability extends to arbitrary spatial dimensions. These results address two major challenges simultaneously and provide important foundations for simulating long-range cold atomic systems.



Figure 1:
Entanglement area law for one-dimensional quantum systems: limitations and generalization.
(Inner box): Short-range interactions and bounded local energies.
Previous rigorous results on the entanglement area law have been limited to systems with short-range interactions and bounded local Hilbert space dimensions or local energies. Paradigmatic examples include spin chains and fermionic models. In these settings, the area law ensures that the entanglement entropy of ground states scales with the boundary, not the volume, of a subsystem.
(Outer box): Long-range interactions and unbounded local energies.
Lifting these foundational constraints is crucial for understanding a broad class of physically relevant systems, such as bosonic quantum fields in cold atom experiments and lattice quantum field theories, where both long-range interactions and unbounded local Hilbert spaces naturally arise. In this work, we rigorously establish the entanglement area law for one-dimensional bosonic systems with power-law decaying interactions and unbounded local energies (Result 2 below), providing a rigorous step toward addressing this longstanding challenge. Our results also provide a theoretical foundation for the efficient and accurate representation of ground states using Matrix Product States (MPS), even in these general settings (Result 3 below).

Introduction

In modern physics, one of the biggest challenges is figuring out how to accurately simulate systems with many interacting particles. This problem shows up in various fields, like condensed matter physics, high-energy physics, and statistical mechanics. To tackle this, researchers study a field called Hamiltonian complexity
[
1
,
2
,
3
]
, which tries to reveal general rules that explain how particles in these systems possess complex structures from the information-theoretic viewpoint. A main focus in this field is the ground state, which is the system’s lowest-energy state at absolute zero temperature, where quantum effects are most pronounced. One of the key discoveries about the ground state is the entanglement area law
[
4
,
5
]
. It states that when a system is divided into two parts, the entanglement entropy scales with the surface area of the partition rather than the volume. The area law is deeply related to the structural complexity of ground states
[
6
]
and is a fundamental ansatz in tensor network algorithms
[
7
,
8
]
, which are some of the most widely used tools for running simulations. Proving the area law and understanding the complexity of ground states have become landmark achievements in quantum information theory.

Over the past two decades, there has been significant progress in our understanding of one-dimensional (1D) ground states. The first rigorous proof of the entanglement area law in 1D systems was provided by Hastings in 2007, marking a major milestone
[
9
]
. Since then, qualitative improvements have been made, particularly by Arad and colleagues, who refined the approach
[
10
,
11
,
12
]
. Additionally, Brandão and collaborators made notable attempts to prove the area law based solely on the exponential decay of correlations in these systems
[
13
,
14
]
. Currently, the proof of the area law for gapped ground states is predominantly achieved through the formalism known as Approximate Ground State Projection (AGSP)
[
11
,
12
]
. This formalism became a crucial stepping stone, leading to the development of a polynomial-time algorithm for computing ground states
[
15
,
16
]
. As of now, 1D systems represent the most successful application of these advancements.

Previous proofs of the area law have primarily relied on two basic conditions
[
9
,
11
,
12
,
17
,
18
]
: i) the short-range interactions, and ii) bounded local energy, as summarized in Fig.
1
.
While these conditions are universally valid in common spin and fermion systems, it is well-known that physical systems violating these conditions are also ubiquitous. As the violation of condition i), long-range interactions are characterized by power-law decay of the interaction strength, which, for instance, have been experimentally observed in cold atomic systems
[
19
,
20
,
21
,
22
,
23
,
24
,
25
,
26
]
. Understanding how these interactions increase complexity compared to short-range interactions has been a fundamental challenge in Hamiltonian complexity
[
27
,
28
,
29
,
30
,
31
,
32
,
33
]
.
On the other hand, as the violation of condition ii), systems with unbounded local energy are well-represented by interacting boson systems. These systems remain largely unexplored, though recent advancements have been made in understanding them from the perspective of information propagation
[
34
,
35
,
36
,
37
]
. The most representative example of interacting boson systems is the Bose-Hubbard model, which serves as the minimal model for describing cold atomic systems
[
38
]
. Another notable example is the
\phi 4
model, a fundamental model in lattice quantum field theory
[
39
,
40
,
41
]
. This model is equivalent to anharmonic oscillators, including nonlinear terms
[
42
,
43
,
44
]
, and is one of the most well-studied models in high-energy theory and statistical mechanics.

Proving the area law in systems that break the aforementioned limitations has long been a major goal, and various studies have been conducted in this direction. For example, in one-dimensional spin systems, generalization to long-range interacting systems has been achieved
[
29
,
45
]
. However, many unresolved aspects remain when it comes to boson systems. This is partly due to the fact that there exist counter-examples that break the area law conjecture, such as the Bose-Hubbard model with attractive interactions.
For example, by considering a Bose-Hubbard model such that

H=J(b_{1}^{\dagger}b_{2}+{\rm h.c.})-V(\hat{n}_{1}+\hat{n}_{2})^{2}+\sum_{i\neq 1,2}U\hat{n}_{i}(\hat{n}_{i}-1)
(
V,U>0
), all the bosons concentrate on the sites
1
and
2
. Hence, as long as the total boson number is proportional to the system size
n
, the entanglement entropy between the site
1
and the other sites can be as large as
\log(n)/2
.
Previous research on the boson area law has been largely limited to non-interacting boson systems
[
46
,
47
]
. Recent breakthroughs have demonstrated that interactions between bosons and other bounded fields (but excluding boson-boson interactions) can be efficiently handled
[
36
]
, from which the area law can be proven under finite-range interactions
[
48
]
.
Still, a unified understanding of generic interacting bosonic systems, encompassing key models such as the Bose-Hubbard and
\phi^{4}
classes, remains a completely open problem.

In this study, we resolve the one-dimensional area law conjecture for general interacting boson systems, including the Bose-Hubbard and
\phi 4
classes. Our results hold even in the presence of long-range interactions, where the interaction decay rate must be faster than
r^{-2}
for the area law to hold. This allows us to establish the area law in the most general cases without the two primary conditions—short-range interactions and bounded local energy. Our results quantitatively show how the boson parameters and long-range interaction parameters, such as the power-law decay rate, influence the entanglement structures [see Ineq. (
13
) below]. In addition, we provide a general upper bound on the bond dimension required to describe these ground states using Matrix Product States (MPS).
Our result serves as a theoretical foundation for simulating long-range interacting cold atomic systems,
which constitute a major platform for modern quantum technologies
[
49
,
50
]
.

A central challenge in analyzing interacting bosonic systems is the infinite dimensionality of the local Hilbert space. Indeed, as highlighted in several prior works
[
39
,
34
,
36
,
37
]
, providing a rigorous and general framework for dimension reduction—with explicit control over the induced error—has long been recognized as one of the most fundamental and difficult tasks in the field. To rigorously establish the area law, it is essential to quantitatively control the ground-state boson number distribution and to implement dimension reduction in a controlled way. Although truncating the local Hilbert space is a standard prescription in both numerical and theoretical studies, it has traditionally been applied without rigorous guarantees on its validity or accuracy.
One of our main technical contributions is to develop a general framework for dimension reduction that can accommodate different types of boson statistics and interaction structures. In particular, we establish analytic tools tailored to both the Bose-Hubbard and the
\phi^{4}
models (Results 1.1 and 1.2 below), each of which requires distinct theoretical approaches due to their differing physical and mathematical structures.

Crucially, when combining long-range interactions with bosonic degrees of freedom, an additional layer of difficulty arises. In such systems, a straightforward dimension reduction fails because preserving the ground state demands an excessively large cutoff. As a result, energy and entanglement entropy grow logarithmically with the cutoff, making the area law inapplicable.
To address this, our framework implements a site-dependent and carefully controlled dimension reduction scheme. We further reconstruct and generalize the effective Hamiltonian theory developed in Ref.
[
51
]
, adapting it to account for the non-uniformity and correlations induced by long-range interactions. This allows us to rigorously maintain the validity of the area law even for bosonic systems with power-law decaying interactions, overcoming obstacles that are absent in either short-range or spin systems.

Our work opens the unified theoretical framework capable of addressing the combined challenges of long-range interactions and unbounded local energy in quantum many-body systems. By integrating these two sources of complexity, our approach enables rigorous analysis and simulation techniques that were previously out of reach.

Results

System setup

We here describe the system setups for the main results that will be shown in the subsequent sections.
We consider a quantum system comprising
n
sites in an arbitrary-dimensional lattice and denote the set of total sites by
\Lambda
, where
|\Lambda|=n
.
We denote the boson creation and annihilation operators at a site
i\in\Lambda
by
b^{\dagger}_{i}
and
b_{i}
, respectively.
Then, the most general form of the interacting boson Hamiltonians up to
k
-th degree is given by:

H={\mathcal{F}}_{H}(\mathbf{b},\mathbf{b}^{\dagger})=\sum_{Z:|Z|\leq k}h_{Z}(\mathbf{b}_{Z},\mathbf{b}_{Z}^{\dagger}),

(1)

where
{\mathcal{F}}_{H}(\mathbf{b},\mathbf{b}^{\dagger})
is an arbitrary
k
-th degree polynomial of
\mathbf{b}=\{b_{i}\}_{i\in\Lambda}
and
\mathbf{b}^{\dagger}=\{b^{\dagger}_{i}\}_{i\in\Lambda}
, and
h_{Z}(\mathbf{b}_{Z},\mathbf{b}_{Z}^{\dagger})
with
\mathbf{b}_{Z}=\{b_{i}\}_{i\in Z}
acts on the subset
Z\subset\Lambda
.

We define
\Pi_{i,<N}
as the projection operator onto the space such that the boson number at the site
i
is smaller than
N
.
As a generalization, we denote
\Pi_{\Lambda,<N}
by
\Pi_{\Lambda,<N}=\bigotimes_{i\in\Lambda}\Pi_{i,<N}
, which truncates the boson number at each site to below
N
.
We adopt the parameter
g
such that

\sum_{Z:Z\ni i}\left\|h_{Z}(\mathbf{b}_{Z},\mathbf{b}_{Z}^{\dagger})\Pi_{\Lambda,<N}\right\|\leq gN^{k/2}

(2)

with
g=\mathcal{O}(1)
; that is, as long as the boson number is truncated by
\mathcal{O}(1)
, their interaction is also upper-bounded by an
\mathcal{O}(1)
constant.
We denote the ground state and the spectral gap by
|\Omega\rangle
and
\Delta
, where we assume the non-degeneracy of the ground-state energy.

Our purpose is to derive a general upper bound for the entanglement entropy
S_{L}(\Omega)
for any bipartition of the total system as
\Lambda=L\sqcup R
.
The entanglement entropy is described by
-{\rm tr}\left(\rho_{L}\log(\rho_{L})\right)
with
\rho_{L}
the reduced density matrix of the ground state on the subset
L
.

Bose-Hubbard classes

The Bose-Hubbard class is represented as follows in an integrated manner:

H=H_{p}(\mathbf{b},\mathbf{b}^{\dagger})+\sum_{i\in\Lambda}U_{i}\hat{n}_{i}^{k/2}\quad(U_{i}>0,\ \forall i\in\Lambda),

(3)

where
\hat{n}_{i}=b_{i}^{\dagger}b_{i}
, and
H_{p}(\mathbf{b},\mathbf{b}^{\dagger})
is an arbitrary
p
th degree polynomial of
\mathbf{b},\mathbf{b}^{\dagger}
with
p\leq k-1
.
Now,
k
denotes the highest degree of the boson number (potential) terms, while
p
indicates the highest degree among off-diagonal terms. It is important to emphasize that our class of Hamiltonians generally allows for particle-number non-conserving terms, including those responsible for pair creation and annihilation processes (e.g.,
\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}}J_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}}(b_{i_{1}}b_{i_{2}}b_{i_{3}}b_{i_{4}}+{\rm h.c.})
with
p=4
). This goes beyond the standard Bose-Hubbard model and encompasses a much wider variety of physically relevant interactions.
For the Bose–Hubbard class, representative gapped phases include the Mott insulating phase at integer filling and the density-wave insulating phase stabilized by repulsive density–density interactions between neighboring sites.

We note that the standard Bose-Hubbard model, i.e.,

H=\sum_{i,i^{\prime}}J_{i,i^{\prime}}(b_{i}b_{i^{\prime}}^{\dagger}+{\rm h.c.})+\sum_{i\in\Lambda}U\hat{n}_{i}(\hat{n}_{i}-1)

(4)

with
U>0
,
corresponds to the case of
k=4
with
p=2
, and the above condition is satisfied.
If we consider the case of
p=k
in Eq. (
3
), there exists a competition between the repulsive and attractive interactions of bosons, and hence, the condition becomes more nontrivial (see Assumption 1 in Supplementary Note 2).
For example, in the Hamiltonian
J(b_{i}^{2}b_{j}^{2}+\mathrm{h.c.})+U(n_{i}^{2}+n_{j}^{2})
, whether the ground state is well-defined depends on the relative strength of
J
and
U
: if
J
becomes large, the attractive effect can dominate and potentially lead to a divergent boson number in the ground state.

We emphasize that our analysis is restricted to the repulsive regime, where on-site interactions suppress clustering tendencies and keep the boson number suitably controlled. In contrast, attractive interactions can favor the accumulation of particles on a single site, leading to collapse- or clustering-type behavior. Such a gathering qualitatively changes the boson number distribution, preventing the effective Hilbert space dimension from remaining small, and may invalidate the concentration bounds underlying our proof. Consequently, the attractive regime admits ground states with a qualitatively different entanglement structure from the repulsive case, and there exist ground states that violate the entanglement area law.

Notably, our model includes interaction-induced tunneling terms of the form
b_{i}(\hat{n}_{i}+\hat{n}_{i^{\prime}})b_{i^{\prime}}^{\dagger}
, where the tunneling amplitude depends explicitly on the occupation numbers. Such interactions are known to violate the Lieb-Robinson bound (Theorem 3 in Ref.
[
52
]
), meaning that information propagation can, in principle, occur at infinite speed.
Surprisingly, however, our results show that even in the presence of these interactions—where dynamical locality is lost—the ground state can still satisfy the entanglement area law. This highlights a fundamental distinction between the locality of information propagation (dynamical locality) and the static locality properties of ground-state entanglement.
In fact, the Lieb-Robinson bound evaluated with respect to a specific state, such as a gapped ground state, can exhibit much slower effective propagation than the state-independent bound, and its violation is determined by a different criterion. This state-dependent perspective on dynamical locality helps explain why a distinction emerges between the loss of general dynamical locality and the possibility that the ground-state entanglement can still remain at an area-law scale.

\phi 4
classes

For the
\phi 4
classes, we introduce the
\phi
operator and
\pi
operator as

\phi=\frac{b+b^{\dagger}}{\sqrt{2}}\and\pi=-i\frac{b-b^{\dagger}}{\sqrt{2}}.

(5)

They correspond to the position and momentum operators, respectively.
Then, we treat the following general Hamiltonian as the
\phi 4
class:

H=\sum_{i\in\Lambda}\mu_{i}\pi_{i}^{2}+{\mathcal{F}}(\bm{\phi}),

(6)

where
\{\mu_{i}\}_{i\in\Lambda}
are real parameters. Throughout this work, we restrict attention to parameter regimes in which the Hamiltonian is bounded from below and admits a unique gapped ground state.
Also,
{\mathcal{F}}(\bm{\phi})
is an arbitrary
k
-degree even function of
\bm{\phi}=\{\phi_{i}\}_{i\in\Lambda}
, e.g.,
\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}}f_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}}\phi_{i_{1}}\phi_{i_{2}}\phi_{i_{3}}\phi_{i_{4}}
.
Note that the Hamiltonian satisfies the parity symmetry, i.e., invariant under
\bm{\phi}\to-\bm{\phi}
.
Clearly, the standard
\phi 4
model, i.e.,

H=\sum_{i\in\Lambda}\left(\pi_{i}^{2}+\phi_{i}^{2}+\lambda\phi_{i}^{4}\right)+\gamma\sum_{\left\langle i,i^{\prime}\right\rangle}\phi_{i}\phi_{i^{\prime}},

(7)

is a specific case of the above Hamiltonian (
6
).
Here, the highest-order term of the boson operator is
\sum_{i\in\Lambda}\lambda\phi_{i}^{4}
.
We note that the parity symmetry is a physically essential assumption underlying our analysis.
By forbidding odd-power terms, it prevents bosonic degrees of freedom from concentrating around large-field configurations.
This allows uniform control of boson-number expectation values, which is essential for establishing the concentration bounds used in our proof.
Indeed, if this assumption is violated, explicit counterexamples that break the entanglement area law can be constructed (see the discussion following Assumption 2 in the Supplementary Information).
This assumption is compatible with standard realizations of
\phi 4
-type systems, including superconducting Kerr oscillators, symmetric double-well optical-lattice potentials, and the conventional lattice
\phi 4
field theory, where the effective potential remains even unless an external bias or magnetic field induces a linear term that breaks the symmetry.
In the
\phi 4
classes with the parity symmetry, the gapped phase exhibits the physical character of a massive disordered (paramagnetic) state: the field fluctuates around the symmetric configuration and exhibits no long-range order, so correlations remain short-ranged.

As an analytical challenge, we can see that the term
\phi_{i}^{4}
allows an infinite number of bosons to sit on a single site at the lowest energy state.
Indeed, even with an infinitesimally small perturbation, the ground state of the
\phi 4
model becomes unstable in the sense that an infinite number of bosons sit on a single site. For example, if we consider a single-site model (
n=1
) with the Hamiltonian
\phi^{4}+\phi^{2}+\pi^{2}+\epsilon(b^{\dagger}\hat{n}b^{\dagger}+b\hat{n}b)
for an arbitrary
\epsilon>0
, the ground state is not well-defined due to the instability.
This prohibits any perturbative approaches to analyze the boson number distribution in the
\phi 4
class, and hence we employ completely different analyses from the case of the Bose-Hubbard class.

As a nontrivial feature of the
\phi^{4}
model, only the existence of a spectral gap guarantees low boson density at each site, but only in the presence of parity symmetry. In contrast, without parity symmetry, a spectral gap alone cannot prevent the accumulation of an extensive number of bosons on a single site. This underlines the crucial role of symmetry in ensuring the stability and localization properties of the ground state (see also the discussion below Theorem 2 in Supplementary Note 5).

Bosonic concentration bound

As our first main result, we show the concentration bound on the boson number distribution at an arbitrary site, where the tail of the distribution is characterized by (sub)exponential decay.
In practical numerical simulations of bosonic systems, it is unavoidable to truncate the local boson number at each site, based on the empirical observation that the occupation number distribution decays rapidly. However, such truncations have traditionally lacked rigorous error bounds or theoretical justification. The concentration bounds established in this section provide a systematic and quantitative foundation for dimension reduction in bosonic models: they ensure that the numerical truncation is both justified and accurate.

We stress that all results presented in this section apply to bosonic systems of arbitrary spatial dimension and remain valid even when the interactions are long-range. Thus, our concentration bounds provide rigorous guarantees for a wide class of quantum lattice models far beyond conventional settings.

Result 1.1.

For the Bose-Hubbard class (Eq. (
3
)), we prove the following inequality:

\langle\Omega|\Pi_{i,\geq x}|\Omega\rangle\leq e^{-2(x-M_{i,0})/k},

(8)

where
M_{i,0}
is an
\mathcal{O}(1)
constant as shown in Eq. (S.151) of Corollary 12 in Supplementary Note 4.
Here, the concentration bound is independent of the spectral gap, and the decay rate is characterized by exponential decay.
Theorem 1 in Supplementary Note 4 generally treats the case of
p=k
in Eq. (
3
), where the repulsive and attractive interactions have the same order.

Result 1.2.

On the other hand, for the
\phi 4
class (Eq. (
6
)), we have a (sub)exponential concentration bound as follows (Theorem 2 in Supplementary Note 5):

\langle\Omega|\Pi_{i,>x}|\Omega\rangle\leq 4e^{k}e^{-kx^{1/k}/(8e\tilde{C})},

(9)

where
\tilde{C}
is a constant proportional to
1/\sqrt{\Delta}
[see Eq. (S.224) in Supplementary Note 5].
In the
\phi 4
class, the concentration bound depends on the spectral gap
\Delta
.
Here, the variance of the boson number in the ground state scales as
\Delta^{-k/2}
, which diverges in the limit of
\Delta\to 0
.

It is worth noting that the qualitative difference in the decay behavior between the Bose-Hubbard and
\phi^{4}
models does not arise from any simple physical intuition. Instead, it reflects the fundamentally different mathematical and physical structures of the two models, which require distinct analytical techniques (see Supplementary Note 5). In particular, the sub-exponential decay observed in the
\phi^{4}
model (Eq. (
7
)) appears to be an intrinsic feature of the model, as revealed by our analysis and further supported by numerical calculations for single-site systems.

1D entanglement area law

Our second main result establishes an entanglement area law for 1D systems under the assumption of a general boson number concentration bound as follows:

\left\|\Pi_{i,>N}|\Omega\rangle\right\|\leq\mathfrak{c}e^{-\mathfrak{b}N^{1/\mathfrak{a}}}\quad\textrm{for}\quad\forall i\in\Lambda,

(10)

where
\mathfrak{b}
depends on the spectral gap as
\mathfrak{b}\propto\Delta^{2\upsilon/(\mathfrak{a}k)}
with
\upsilon
a constant that depends on the system detail (see Supplementary Note 6).
This bound enables us to effectively reduce the local Hilbert space dimension and control the entanglement entropy, even in the presence of unbounded bosonic degrees of freedom and long-range interactions.
It is worth noting that we here consider the most general Hamiltonian in Eq. (
1
) and do not restrict ourselves to any specific model, such as the Hamiltonian classes in Eqs. (
3
) and (
6
).

For the Hamiltonian in Eq. (
1
), we allow general power-law decaying interactions.
We characterize the decay rate by

\left\|h_{Z}(\mathbf{b}_{Z},\mathbf{b}_{Z}^{\dagger})\Pi_{\Lambda,<N}\right\|\leq J_{Z}N^{k/2},

(11)

and

\sum_{Z:Z\ni\{i,i^{\prime}\}}|J_{Z}|\leq g\bar{J}(d_{i,i^{\prime}}),

(12)

where
\bar{J}(r)
polynomially decays with the distance
r
as
r^{-\alpha}
(
\alpha>2
).

Result 2.

Under the above conditions, we prove the following upper bound on the entanglement entropy for an arbitrary bipartition
\Lambda=L\cup R
(Theorem 3 in Supplementary Note 6):

S_{L}(\Omega)\leq C_{0}{\mathcal{G}}_{\bar{\alpha},\upsilon,\chi}(\Delta)

(13)

with
\chi=k\mathfrak{a}/2
and
\bar{\alpha}=\alpha-2
, where

{\mathcal{G}}_{\bar{\alpha},\upsilon,\chi}(\Delta)=\Delta^{-(1+2/\bar{\alpha})(\upsilon+1)}\left[\log\left(1/\Delta\right)\right]^{4+3/\bar{\alpha}+\chi(1+2/\bar{\alpha})},

(14)

and
C_{0}
is an
\mathcal{O}(1)
constant.
Here,
\bar{\alpha}
characterizes the decay of the long-range interaction, while
\chi
and
\upsilon
capture the influence of the bosonic statistics and the spectral gap, respectively.

The derived area law in Eq. (
13
) depends on three parameters:
\bar{\alpha}
,
\chi
, and
\upsilon
. The parameter
\bar{\alpha}
characterizes the strength of the long-range interaction, while
\chi
and
\upsilon
capture the bosonic properties. In the limits
\chi\to 0
and
\upsilon\to 0
, we recover the result for the long-range area law of spin systems from Ref.
[
29
]
[see the inequality (S.351) in Supplementary Note 6, where
\upsilon=0
is specifically considered]. Additionally, in the limit
\bar{\alpha}\to\infty
, our area law reduces to the original area law for short-range spin systems from Ref.
[
12
]
. This shows that our area law is a natural extension of the previous results, as it reduces to them in the appropriate limits.

Moreover, our results provide a rigorous efficiency guarantee for approximating the ground state with a Matrix Product State (MPS), a class of variational ansatzes that underlies many powerful tensor network algorithms for simulating quantum many-body systems.

Result 3.
We prove that there exists an MPS
|{\rm M}_{{\mathcal{D}}}\rangle
that approximates the ground state in the sense that

\left\|{\rm tr}_{X^{\rm c}}\left(|\Omega\rangle\langle\Omega|-|{\rm M}_{{\mathcal{D}}}\rangle\langle{\rm M}_{{\mathcal{D}}}|\right)\right\|_{1}\leq\delta|X|

(15)

for
\forall X\subseteq\Lambda
by choosing the bond dimension
{\mathcal{D}}
as

{\mathcal{D}}=e^{C_{1}{\mathcal{G}}_{\bar{\alpha},\upsilon,\chi}(\Delta)+C_{2}\Delta^{-(\upsilon+1)/2}\log^{\chi/2+5/2}\left(\frac{1}{\delta\Delta}\right)},

(16)

where
C_{1},C_{2}
are
\mathcal{O}(1)
constants and the error
\delta
is arbitrarily chosen.
In particular, for polynomially small error
\delta=1/\mathrm{poly}(n)
and constant gap
\Delta=\mathcal{O}(1)
, the required bond dimension
{\mathcal{D}}
scales only quasi-polynomially with system size
n
:

{\mathcal{D}}=\exp\left[C^{\prime}_{2}\log^{\chi/2+5/2}(n)\right],\quad C^{\prime}_{2}=\mathcal{O}(1).

(17)

If a many-body quantum state defined on
n
sites admits a matrix product state representation with bond dimension
\mathcal{D}
, tensor-network algorithms evaluate local observables with cost
\mathcal{O}(n\mathcal{D}^{3})
; thus, a quasi-polynomial bound on
\mathcal{D}
implies classical simulability with complexity close to polynomial time.
This result provides a rigorous guarantee for the efficiency of tensor network algorithms in simulating interacting bosonic systems, even when both long-range interactions and unbounded local Hilbert spaces are present.

By matching the parameters in the general concentration bound (Eq. (
10
)) to the explicit forms obtained for the Bose-Hubbard (Eq. (
8
)) and
\phi^{4}
(Eq. (
9
)) classes, we set

\{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{c},\upsilon\}=\{1,1/k,e^{M_{i,0}/k},0\}
for the Bose-Hubbard class, and

\{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{c},\upsilon\}=\{k,k/(16e\tilde{C}),2e^{k/2},k^{2}/4\}
for the
\phi^{4}
class, where we use
\left\|\Pi_{i,>N}|\Omega\rangle\right\|=\langle\Omega|\Pi_{i,>N}|\Omega\rangle^{1/2}
.
This yields explicit area law statements for both models [see the inequalities (S.354) and (S.356) in Supplementary Note 6 for the explicit forms].

Discussions

In this work, we have established the entanglement area law and MPS efficiency guarantees for general interacting bosonic systems, including those with long-range interactions and unbounded local energies. Our results provide theoretical foundations for the validity of existing numerical methods in studying the Bose-Hubbard models
[
53
,
54
]
and related quantum field theories
[
55
,
56
]
.

There are still several open questions. In the Bose-Hubbard class, we have focused on repulsive interactions, leaving the entanglement structure in the attractive case largely unknown. For models with conserved total boson number (e.g., the standard Bose-Hubbard model), there is a trivial logarithmic violation of the entanglement area law. However, it is unclear whether (sub)volume entanglement scaling can occur under a spectral gap assumption.
In the
\phi^{4}
class, it remains an open problem to remove the parity symmetry assumption. As shown in Supplementary Information (Theorem 2 therein), a finite bound on the position operator
\{\phi_{i}\}_{i\in\Lambda}
enables the derivation of a concentration bound (Eq. (
9
)). A key challenge lies in proving this boundedness under only the spectral gap assumption.

Furthermore, the concentration bounds on the boson number probability apply to systems in arbitrary dimensions.
Although the area law conjecture is still intractable in high dimensions, our results provide an essential foundation for future resolutions of the area law in high-dimensional boson systems.
At the moment, by imposing additional assumptions—such as the existence of an adiabatic path to non-interacting models—we expect to prove a bosonic area law in higher dimensions by employing the Small-Incremental-Entangling (SIE) theorem
[
57
]
.

Finally, developing a quasi-polynomial-time algorithm for simulating interacting boson systems with long-range interactions is also an important goal. Currently, efficient algorithms for one-dimensional ground states exist only for Hamiltonians with short-range interactions and bounded local energy
[
15
,
16
]
. Extending these algorithms to the general setting and resolving the time complexity for generic interacting boson systems remains a major open problem in quantum many-body physics.

Among the open directions, the most tractable with current techniques appears to be establishing an area law in higher dimensions under the assumption of an adiabatic path to a trivial gapped system.
Since our work already proves suppression of boson number distributions in arbitrary dimensions, combining this with the SIE theorem provides a concrete route toward such a generalization.
Furthermore, beyond the entropy itself, analyzing the dynamics of the full entanglement spectrum may reduce the quasi-polynomial complexity of long-range interacting boson systems to polynomial complexity
[
58
]
, providing a potential pathway toward the development of polynomial-time algorithms for interacting bosonic systems.

Methods

Proof sketch of Result 1.1 (Bose-Hubbard class)

We outline the proof of the exponential concentration bound (Eq. (
8
)) for the Bose-Hubbard class of Hamiltonians (See Fig.
2
).

Figure 2:
Proof strategy for the exponential boson-number concentration bound in the Bose-Hubbard class.
The diagram shows how the proof of Result 1.1 converts local repulsion into an exponential tail bound for the boson-number distribution.
The argument first bounds the non-diagonal and density-dependent parts of the Hamiltonian by the highest power of the local number operator. In the repulsive regime, this yields a uniform
\mathcal{O}(1)
bound on the ground-state maximal local moment
\mathcal{Q}_{\Omega}
.
It then establishes an energetic penalty for any state with a large maximal local moment
\mathcal{Q}
, showing that such states lie high above the ground-state energy
E_{0}
.
Next, the ground state is decomposed into boson-number intervals
I_{m}
at a fixed site, with weights
p_{m}
and normalized components
|\omega_{m}\rangle
.
Because the Hamiltonian changes the local occupation by at most
k
, only neighboring intervals are coupled.
Lower bounds on the interval-resolved moments
\mathcal{Q}_{m}
, together with the variational principle applied to the state with the
m
th component removed, yield a recurrence relation for
\{p_{m}\}
.
Solving this recurrence gives an exponential bound on
p_{m}
and hence an exponential decay of the local boson-number tail, establishing Result 1.1.

We first provide a more precise setup.
The Bose-Hubbard Hamiltonian (Eq. (
3
)) can be decomposed into three distinct components:

H=H_{0}(\mathbf{b},\mathbf{b}^{\dagger})+V_{+}(\mathbf{n})+\sum_{i\in\Lambda}U_{i}\hat{n}_{i}^{k/2},

(18)

where the first term,
H_{0}(\mathbf{b},\mathbf{b}^{\dagger})
, is a polynomial of degree at most
k
in the bosonic annihilation and creation operators
\mathbf{b}=\{b_{i}\}_{i\in\Lambda}
and
\mathbf{b}^{\dagger}=\{b_{i}^{\dagger}\}_{i\in\Lambda}
, typically representing particle hopping processes or other interaction terms with possibly negative contributions.
Its explicit form is given by

\displaystyle H_{0}(\mathbf{b},\mathbf{b}^{\dagger})=

\displaystyle\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{k-1}\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k_{1}}\in\Lambda}\sum_{s=1}^{k_{1}+1}J_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k_{1}}}^{(s)}

(19)

\displaystyle\times b_{i_{k_{1}}}^{\dagger}\cdots b_{i_{s+1}}^{\dagger}b_{i_{s}}^{\dagger}b_{i_{s-1}}\cdots b_{i_{2}}b_{i_{1}}.

The second term,
V_{+}(\mathbf{n})
, is a positive semi-definite polynomial of degree at most
k/2
in the local number operators
\mathbf{n}=\{\hat{n}_{i}\}_{i\in\Lambda}
.
The third term represents the leading-order local repulsive interaction,
\sum_{i\in\Lambda}U_{i}\hat{n}_{i}^{k/2}
, which contains the highest-degree (order
k
) contributions in the bosonic operators.

For notational convenience, we define for any region
X\subset\Lambda
the restricted and supported Hamiltonians by

H_{X}:=\sum_{Z\subset X}h_{Z},\quad\widehat{H_{X}}:=\sum_{Z:Z\cap X\neq\emptyset}h_{Z},

(20)

and adopt the same definitions analogously for the components
H_{0}
and
V_{+}
.
To analyze local contributions from
H_{0}
, using the definition
\left|O\right|:=\sqrt{O^{\dagger}O}
of the modulus of an operator
O
, we define
\overline{H_{0,i}}(\mathbf{b},\mathbf{b}^{\dagger})
as the modulus of the site-supported component
\widehat{H_{0,i}}(\mathbf{b},\mathbf{b}^{\dagger})
:

\overline{H_{0,i}}(\mathbf{b},\mathbf{b}^{\dagger})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\ldots,i_{k_{1}}\in\Lambda\\
\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k_{1}}\}\ni i\end{subarray}}\sum_{s=1}^{k_{1}+1}\left|J_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k_{1}}}^{(s)}\right|\cdot\left|b_{i_{k_{1}}}^{\dagger}\cdots b_{i_{s+1}}^{\dagger}b_{i_{s}}^{\dagger}b_{i_{s-1}}\cdots b_{i_{2}}b_{i_{1}}\right|.

(21)

To quantify the strength of this interaction, we introduce the parameter
\bar{J}_{i,k_{1}}
as follows:

\bar{J}_{i,k_{1}}:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\ldots,i_{k_{1}}\in\Lambda\\
\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k_{1}}\}\ni i\end{subarray}}\sum_{s=1}^{k_{1}}\left|J_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k_{1}}}^{(s)}\right|.

(22)

This picks up all the interaction coefficients that include the site
i
.
We assume
U_{i}>5\bar{J}_{i,k}
for all
i\in\Lambda
, where
\bar{J}_{i,k}
is defined by setting
k_{1}=k
in Eq. (
22
).
This ensures that the leading-order repulsion dominates (see Assumption 1 in the Supplementary Information).
If
H_{0}
contains terms only up to degree
k-1
as has been shown in Eq. (
3
), then
\bar{J}_{i,k}=0
and we only have to assume
U_{i}>0
.

The first core idea of the proof is to initiate the analysis from the maximum local moment with respect to the ground state
|\Omega\rangle
of the Hamiltonian
H
across the lattice:

\max_{i\in\Lambda}\langle\Omega|\hat{n}_{i}^{k/2}|\Omega\rangle=:\mathcal{Q}_{\Omega}^{k/2},

(23)

which serves as a central quantity for bounding the concentration behavior.
We aim to clarify the following two points.

1.

In the ground state, the quantity
\mathcal{Q}_{\Omega}
is upper-bounded by a constant value as in the inequality (
27
) below.

2.

Any quantum state with a large maximum local moment must have a large energy, as will be shown in the inequality (
28
).

We begin with the first point.
Without loss of generality, we label the site
i=1
such that
\langle\hat{n}_{1}^{k/2}\rangle=\mathcal{Q}_{\Omega}^{k/2}
, and denote its complement by
\Lambda_{1}:=\Lambda\setminus\{1\}
. The Hamiltonian can then be decomposed as
H=\widehat{H}_{1}+H_{\Lambda_{1}}
where the supported part
\widehat{H_{1}}
admits the decomposition
\widehat{H_{1}}=\widehat{H_{0,1}}(\mathbf{b},\mathbf{b}^{\dagger})+\widehat{V_{+,1}}(\mathbf{n})+U_{1}\hat{n}_{1}^{k/2}
.
Since
|\Omega\rangle
is the ground state of
H
, the variational principle implies

\langle\Omega|\widehat{H}_{1}|\Omega\rangle\leq\delta_{*}\mathcal{Q}_{\Omega}^{(k-1)/2},

(24)

where
\delta_{*}=0
when the total boson number is not conserved (see Eq. (S.90) in the Supplementary Information), and
\delta_{*}\propto(N/|\Lambda|)^{k/2}

when the total boson number is fixed to
N
(see Eq. (S.111) and Eq. (S.132) in the Supplementary Information).
Here, we note that the analysis required to obtain the above inequality becomes more complicated in the total-boson-number-conserving case than in the nonconserving case.
In the latter situation, the local occupation numbers can fluctuate independently, which allows us to construct simple variational reference states when applying the variational principle for the ground-state energy.
In contrast, when the total boson number is conserved, the allowed variational states must satisfy a global constraint on the total particle number.
This constraint correlates local occupation numbers across different sites, making the construction of suitable variational states more involved.
Using the bounds
\widehat{H_{0,1}}(\mathbf{b},\mathbf{b}^{\dagger})\succeq-\overline{H_{0,1}}(\mathbf{b},\mathbf{b}^{\dagger})
and
\widehat{V_{+,1}}(\mathbf{n})\succeq 0
, we obtain

\displaystyle\delta_{*}\mathcal{Q}_{\Omega}^{(k-1)/2}

\displaystyle\geq\langle\Omega|\widehat{H}_{1}|\Omega\rangle

(25)

\displaystyle\geq U_{1}\langle\Omega|\hat{n}_{1}^{k/2}|\Omega\rangle-\langle\Omega|\overline{H_{0,1}}(\mathbf{b},\mathbf{b}^{\dagger})|\Omega\rangle.

Exploiting the extremality of
\mathcal{Q}_{\Omega}^{k/2}
, we derive the following bound on
\overline{H_{0,1}}
(see Proposition 4 in the Supplementary Information):

\langle\Omega|\overline{H_{0,1}}(\mathbf{b},\mathbf{b}^{\dagger})|\Omega\rangle\leq\bar{J}_{1,k}\,\mathcal{Q}_{\Omega}^{k/2}+2\bar{\mathcal{J}}\,\mathcal{Q}_{\Omega}^{(k-1)/2},

(26)

where the quantity
\bar{\mathcal{J}}
is defined as
\bar{\mathcal{J}}:=\max_{i\in\Lambda}\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{k}\bar{J}_{i,k_{1}}\left(1+(2k_{1})^{k_{1}}\right)
.
Substituting Eq. (
26
) into Eq. (
25
), we obtain the following upper bound on
\mathcal{Q}_{\Omega}
(see Propositions 6 and 10 in the Supplementary Information):

\mathcal{Q}_{\Omega}\leq\max\left\{1,\left(\frac{\delta_{*}+2\bar{\mathcal{J}}}{U_{1}-\bar{J}_{1,k}}\right)^{2}\right\}.

(27)

This implies that the
k/2
-th moment of the local boson number operator is bounded by an
\mathcal{O}(1)
constant in the ground state, provided that the total boson number satisfies
N=\mathcal{O}(|\Lambda|)
.

Conversely, we establish an energetic penalty for large local moments.
Specifically, let us consider the lowest possible energy
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}}
among all states whose maximal local
k/2
-th moment satisfies
\max_{i\in\Lambda}\langle\Psi|\hat{n}_{i}^{k/2}|\Psi\rangle=\mathcal{Q}^{k/2}
.
We show that if
\mathcal{Q}
is large, then the energy difference
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}}-E_{0}
above the ground state grows rapidly with
\mathcal{Q}
.
In other words, quantum states exhibiting large local boson-number moments are necessarily separated from the ground state by a large energy gap, and therefore, such states are only possible at high energy above the ground state.
More precisely (see Corollaries 7 and 11 in the Supplementary Information),

\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}}-E_{0}\geq\left(U_{1}-\bar{J}_{1,k}\right)\mathcal{Q}^{k/2}-\left(\delta_{*}+2\bar{\mathcal{J}}\right)\mathcal{Q}^{(k-1)/2}.

(28)

The second crucial idea is to partition the boson number into several intervals and derive a recurrence relation for the concentration probability within each interval, based on the previously established energy bounds.
We divide the nonnegative real line into intervals as follows: let
I_{0}:=[0,M)
,
I_{m}:=[M+(m-1)k,\,M+mk)
for
1\leq m<\bar{m}
,
I_{\bar{m}}:=[M+(\bar{m}-1)k,\,\infty)
for some control parameter
M\in\mathbb{N}
.

Using these intervals, we decompose the ground state
|\Omega\rangle
via projections
\Pi_{1,I_{m}}
, which project onto the subspace where the boson number at site
i=1
lies in
I_{m}
. This yields:

|\Omega\rangle=\sum_{m=0}^{\bar{m}}\Pi_{1,I_{m}}|\Omega\rangle=\sum_{m=0}^{\bar{m}}p_{m}^{1/2}|\omega_{m}\rangle,

(29)

where
p_{m}:=\langle\Omega|\Pi_{1,I_{m}}|\Omega\rangle
represents the probability of the boson number at site 1 falling within the interval
I_{m}
, and the normalized vector is defined as
|\omega_{m}\rangle:=p_{m}^{-1/2}\Pi_{1,I_{m}}|\Omega\rangle
.
Since the Hamiltonian can only change the local boson number by at most
k
, the constructed intervals ensure that

\Pi_{1,I_{m}}H\Pi_{1,I_{m^{\prime}}}=0\qquad\text{for all}\quad|m-m^{\prime}|>1,

(30)

i.e., the Hamiltonian does not couple nonadjacent intervals.

As in the previous argument, we define

\mathcal{Q}_{m}^{k/2}:=\max_{i\in\Lambda}\langle\omega_{m}|\hat{n}_{i}^{k/2}|\omega_{m}\rangle,

(31)

which quantifies the maximum local
k/2
-moment within each component state
|\omega_{m}\rangle
. By construction, for all
m\geq 1
,

\mathcal{Q}_{m}^{k/2}\geq(M+mk-k)^{k/2}.

(32)

We choose the parameter
M\in\mathbb{N}
such that the initial weight satisfies
p_{0}\geq 1/2
, and the corresponding moment bound obeys (see Lemma 13 in the Supplementary Information)

\mathcal{Q}_{0}^{k/2}\leq 2\left(\frac{\delta_{*}+2\bar{\mathcal{J}}}{U_{i}-\bar{J}_{i,k}}\right)^{k}.

(33)

The final goal is to derive a recurrence relation among the weights
\{p_{m}\}_{m=0}^{\bar{m}}
, using the moment bound on
\mathcal{Q}_{m}
to control the tail distribution of the local boson number.

To investigate the decay of the concentration tail, we consider the modified state

|\Omega^{\neq m}\rangle:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-p_{m}}}\sum_{s\neq m}p_{s}^{1/2}|\omega_{s}\rangle,

(34)

which is obtained from the ground state
|\Omega\rangle
by removing the
|\omega_{m}\rangle
component. We define the associated energy difference as

p_{m}\Delta E_{m}:=E_{0}-(1-p_{m})\langle\Omega^{\neq m}|H|\Omega^{\neq m}\rangle,

(35)

where
\Delta E_{m}\leq E_{0}
follows from the variational principle.
Due to the fact that the Hamiltonian only couples adjacent intervals, the quantity
p_{m}\Delta E_{m}
depends solely on the states
|\omega_{m}\rangle
and
|\omega_{m\pm 1}\rangle
. Substituting the decompositions of
|\Omega\rangle
and
|\Omega^{\neq m}\rangle
, and applying the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, we obtain the following lower bound:

p_{m}\Delta E_{m}\geq p_{m}\langle\omega_{m}|H|\omega_{m}\rangle-2\sum_{x=\pm 1}\sqrt{p_{m}p_{m+x}}\left(\langle\omega_{m+x}|\overline{H_{0,1}}|\omega_{m+x}\rangle\langle\omega_{m}|\overline{H_{0,1}}|\omega_{m}\rangle\right)^{1/2}.

(36)

Analogously to Eq. (
26
), we use the bound

\displaystyle\langle\omega_{m}|\overline{H_{0,1}}|\omega_{m}\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\bar{J}_{1,k}\mathcal{Q}_{m}^{k/2}+2\bar{\mathcal{J}}\mathcal{Q}_{m}^{(k-1)/2}

(37)

\displaystyle=:T_{m}^{2},\quad\forall m.

Furthermore, from the ground-state energy bound (cf. Eq. (
25
)), we have

\displaystyle\langle\omega_{m}|H|\omega_{m}\rangle

(38)

\displaystyle\geq(U_{1}-\bar{J}_{1,k})\mathcal{Q}_{m}^{k/2}-(\delta_{*}+2\bar{\mathcal{J}})\mathcal{Q}_{m}^{(k-1)/2}+E_{0}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\zeta_{0}}T_{m}^{2}+E_{0}

for some
\mathcal{O}(1)
constant
\zeta_{0}\leq 1/4
(see Eq. (S.179) in the Supplementary Information).
Combining Eqs. (
36
)–(
38
), we obtain the recurrence inequality:

\sqrt{p_{m}}\leq 2\zeta_{0}\left(\sqrt{p_{m+1}}\frac{T_{m+1}}{T_{m}}+\sqrt{p_{m-1}}\frac{T_{m-1}}{T_{m}}\right).

(39)

By solving the recurrence relation (as presented in Lemma 15 of the Supplementary Information) and applying Eqs. (
32
) and (
33
), we obtain

p_{m}\leq\left(\frac{1-\sqrt{1-16\zeta_{0}^{2}}}{4\zeta_{0}}\right)^{2m},

(40)

which establishes the exponential bound of the boson-number concentration in the Bose–Hubbard class.

Proof sketch of Result 1.2 (
\phi 4
class)

We present a sketch of the proof for the subexponential concentration bound (Eq. (
9
)) applicable to the
\phi^{4}
class of Hamiltonians (Eq. (
6
)) (See Fig.
3
). In contrast to the Bose-Hubbard case, our argument here relies on the assumption of a finite spectral gap.
Indeed, if the gap condition is not imposed, there exist models where the ground state allows arbitrarily many bosons to occupy a single site.
To connect the gap condition to the concentration of the boson number distribution, the key idea is to utilize the following universal trade-off inequality between the spectral gap and the variance of an arbitrary operator (cf. Ref.
[
59
]
):

\mathrm{Var}(O)\cdot\Delta\leq\frac{1}{2}\left|\langle\Omega|[[H,O],O]|\Omega\rangle\right|.

(41)

Here,
\mathrm{Var}(O)
denotes the variance of an operator
O
with respect to the ground state
|\Omega\rangle
, defined as
\mathrm{Var}(O)=\langle\Omega|O^{2}|\Omega\rangle-\langle\Omega|O|\Omega\rangle^{2}
, and
\Delta
has been defined as the spectral gap.
This inequality expresses that a finite spectral gap constrains the extent to which an observable can fluctuate in the ground state. In particular, if an operator has a small double commutator with the Hamiltonian, so that it is approximately conserved or only weakly affected by the dynamics, then its ground-state fluctuations must be correspondingly suppressed. Conversely, large fluctuations of an observable necessarily imply that it couples strongly to excitations across the gap, as quantified by its noncommutativity with the Hamiltonian.

We apply the inequality (
41
) to the operator
\phi^{m}
, where we omit the lattice index
i
for simplicity.
In the
\phi^{4}
class of Hamiltonians, fluctuations of the field operator are constrained solely through the kinetic term
\pi^{2}
, which is the only term that mediates the noncommutativity underlying the inequality (
41
).
Evaluating the resulting double commutator using the canonical commutation relation
[\phi,\pi]=i
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}(\phi^{m})

\displaystyle=\left(\langle\Omega|\phi^{2m}|\Omega\rangle-\langle\Omega|\phi^{m}|\Omega\rangle^{2}\right)\cdot\Delta

(42)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\bar{\mu}}{2}\left|\langle\Omega|[[\pi^{2},\phi^{m}],\phi^{m}]|\Omega\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq\bar{\mu}m^{2}\left|\langle\Omega|\phi^{2m-2}|\Omega\rangle\right|,

where
\bar{\mu}:=\max_{i\in\Lambda}|\mu_{i}|
.
As a consequence, ground-state fluctuations of
\phi
are hierarchically constrained, with higher moments recursively bounded in terms of lower ones.
Solving this recurrence (see Proposition 16 in the Supplementary Information) yields the bound

\left|\langle\Omega|\phi^{s}|\Omega\rangle\right|\leq(C_{\phi}s)^{s},

(43)

where
C_{\phi}=\mathcal{O}\left(\sqrt{\bar{\mu}/\Delta}\right)
.

Figure 3:
Proof strategy for the subexponential boson-number concentration bound in the
\phi 4
class.
The diagram illustrates how a finite spectral gap leads to subexponential suppression of large boson-number fluctuations in Result 1.2.
The proof starts from the variance-gap trade-off inequality
\mathrm{Var}(O)\cdot\Delta\leq|\langle\Omega|[[H,O],O]|\Omega\rangle|/2
, which constrains ground-state fluctuations through the spectral gap
\Delta
.
Applying this identity to powers of the field operator
\phi_{i}
recursively bounds higher
\phi
-moments by lower ones and yields
|\langle\Omega|\phi_{i}^{s}|\Omega\rangle|\leq(C_{\phi}s)^{s}
with
C_{\phi}=\mathcal{O}(\Delta^{-1/2})
.
A central technical step is then to control mixed non-commuting moments involving both
\phi
and
\pi
.
For this purpose, we prove a Hölder-type inequality adapted to non-commuting operators, which reduces mixed moments to controllable combinations of
\phi
- and
\pi
-moments.
This closes the recursion for
\pi
-moments and gives
|\langle\Omega|\pi_{i}^{s}|\Omega\rangle|\leq(C_{\pi}s)^{ks/2}
.
Combining the resulting
\phi
- and
\pi
-estimates with
\phi^{2}+\pi^{2}=2\hat{n}+1
yields upper bounds on the moments of the local boson-number operator.
Markov’s inequality then converts these moment bounds into a subexponential tail estimate for the local boson-number distribution, establishing Result 1.2.

A similar overall approach can be applied to the
\pi
operator, but the situation becomes significantly more complicated.
An important distinction arises between the analysis of
\phi
and
\pi
operators.
For the moments of
\phi
, all relevant expectations involve only powers of
\phi
, so the moment bound can be closed solely in terms of
\phi
itself as in (
42
).
In contrast, when estimating moments of
\pi
, the high-order terms with respect to
\phi
inevitably lead to expectation values involving mixed products of both operators, such as
\left|\langle\Omega|\pi^{2m-s}\phi^{s^{\prime}}|\Omega\rangle\right|
.
If one could somehow separate the
\phi
and
\pi
contributions, it would be possible to apply the established moment bounds for
\phi
and derive a closed recurrence inequality for
\pi
.
However, since Hölder’s inequality is not available for non-commuting operators, this separation is highly nontrivial and poses a major technical challenge.
To address this issue, we establish a non-commutative version of Hölder’s inequality (see Proposition 20 in the Supplementary Information), which enables us to control mixed moments of
\pi
and
\phi
in terms of the moments of
\pi
alone.

While the detailed calculations are deferred to the Supplementary Information (see Subtheorem 1 therein), the key outcome is that we ultimately obtain a simple closed-form inequality for the moments of the
\pi
operator, analogous to that for
\phi
:

\left|\langle\Omega|\pi^{s}|\Omega\rangle\right|\leq(C_{\pi}s)^{ks/2},

(44)

where
C_{\pi}=\mathcal{O}\left(\sqrt{\bar{\mu}/\Delta}\right)
.
Finally, using the identity
\phi^{2}+\pi^{2}=2\hat{n}+1
that relates the fields to the boson number operator, we derive (see Proposition 18 in the Supplementary Information) the bound

\langle\Omega|\hat{n}^{s}|\Omega\rangle\leq(Cs)^{ks},

(45)

where
C=\mathcal{O}\left(\sqrt{\bar{\mu}/\Delta}\right)
.

Based on the moment bound of the boson number operator (Eq. (
45
)), we can estimate the tail of its probability distribution via Markov’s inequality:

\langle\Omega|\Pi_{i,>x}|\Omega\rangle\leq\min_{s\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{(Cs)^{ks}}{x^{s}}.

(46)

Choosing the optimizing parameter as
s=\left\lfloor x^{1/k}/(eC)\right\rfloor
, we obtain the subexponential concentration bound for the boson number:

\langle\Omega|\Pi_{i,>x}|\Omega\rangle\lesssim\exp\left(-\frac{k}{eC}x^{1/k}\right),

(47)

which completes the proof.

Proof sketch of Result 2 (entanglement area law)

In this section, we provide a brief summary of the proof of the area law for one-dimensional bosonic systems with long-range interactions (See Fig.
4
). We consider a one-dimensional lattice defined on
\mathbb{Z}
, where each site
i
is labeled by its position
x\in\mathbb{Z}
. The system is bipartitioned into left and right semi-infinite subsystems,
L=(-\infty,0]
and
R=[1,\infty)
, and we study the entanglement entropy of the ground state
|\Omega\rangle
across this bipartition.

Our approach is based on the framework of approximate ground state projectors (AGSPs), originally introduced for systems with short-range interactions
[
11
,
12
]
, and subsequently extended to long-range interacting systems
[
29
]
. An AGSP operator
K
approximates the ground state projector
|\Omega\rangle\langle\Omega|
, exhibiting a trade-off between its Schmidt rank and approximation accuracy (see Supplementary Note 7.A). The central objective is to construct an AGSP with low Schmidt rank and high fidelity, which enables the construction of a low-entangled approximation to the ground state (see Lemma 24 in the Supplementary Information).
In this work, we further extend the AGSP framework to encompass unbounded bosonic Hamiltonians, under the assumption that the boson number distribution exhibits subexponential decay (Eq. (
10
)).

For this purpose, we perform a sequence of controlled truncations that systematically reduce the original long-range, unbounded bosonic Hamiltonian to a bounded, quasi-local, and gapped form suitable for the application of standard area law techniques. Concretely, we transform the original Hamiltonian
H
through the following stages:

\displaystyle H\xrightarrow{\text{boson-number truncation}}

\displaystyle\bar{H}

(48)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\text{interaction truncation}}

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{t}}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\text{multi-energy truncation}}

\displaystyle\tilde{H}_{\mathrm{t}}.

First, a boson-number truncation is performed to bound the local particle number on each site, effectively rendering the local Hilbert space finite-dimensional.
Next, we apply an interaction truncation to remove long-range couplings beyond adjacent blocks near the bipartition boundary, thereby localizing the relevant interactions. Finally, a multi-energy truncation is used to constrain the intra-block energies at a prescribed cutoff, ensuring that the total Hamiltonian becomes bounded.
At each step, the truncation parameters are carefully chosen to control the approximation error and to preserve a positive spectral gap, as rigorously quantified in Propositions 25–26 and Theorem 4 in the Supplementary Information.

Once this reduction is achieved, the resulting Hamiltonian
\tilde{H}_{\mathrm{t}}
is both bounded and quasi-local, allowing us to apply the standard Chebyshev-polynomial-based AGSP construction developed for short-range systems. This ultimately establishes the one-dimensional entanglement area law for the original class of long-range, unbounded bosonic Hamiltonians.

Figure 4:
Proof strategy for the one-dimensional bosonic area law with long-range interactions.
The diagram outlines how the proof of Result 2 reduces a long-range bosonic Hamiltonian with unbounded local Hilbert spaces to a bounded effective model amenable to the approximate ground-state projector (AGSP) method.
The top box recalls the AGSP principle, namely that a sufficiently accurate AGSP with controlled Schmidt rank implies an entanglement area law.
The proof begins from a general
k
-local bosonic Hamiltonian with a subexponentially decaying local boson-number distribution and power-law interactions.
First, a site-dependent boson-number truncation
H\to\bar{H}
is introduced, with cutoffs
N_{x}
growing polylogarithmically with the distance from the bipartition boundary; this controls the local boson number while preserving the ground state and the spectral gap up to controlled error.
Second, an interaction truncation
\bar{H}\to H_{\mathrm{t}}
reduces the Hamiltonian to block-local terms and nearest-neighbor inter-block couplings, with the induced error controlled by the block number
q
, the block length
l
, and the interaction decay.
Third, a block-wise multi-energy truncation
H_{\mathrm{t}}\to\tilde{H}_{\mathrm{t}}
makes the effective Hamiltonian bounded while preserving the low-energy sector up to a subexponentially small error in the cutoff
\tau
.
A Chebyshev-polynomial AGSP construction can then be applied to
\tilde{H}_{\mathrm{t}}
, yielding the quantitative area law in Result 2.

We begin with the first truncation in Eq. (
48
) to address the unbounded local Hilbert space.
A central idea is to introduce a site-dependent boson-number truncation, where the cutoff
N_{x}
grows poly-logarithmically with the distance from the boundary:

\displaystyle\bar{\Pi}=\Pi_{\mathbf{N}}:=\bigotimes_{x\in\Lambda}\Pi_{x,\leq N_{x}},

(49)

\displaystyle N_{x}=\mathcal{O}\left(\log^{\mathfrak{a}}(\epsilon^{-1})+\log^{\mathfrak{a}}\left(|x|^{3}+1\right)\right),

where the parameter
\epsilon
is appropriately chosen below so that the ground state is preserved up to a sufficiently small error.
We then define the truncated Hamiltonian as
\bar{H}:=\bar{\Pi}H\bar{\Pi}
. This choice of
N_{x}
ensures that both the spectral gap
\bar{\Delta}
and the ground state
|\bar{\Omega}\rangle
of
\bar{H}
approximate those of the original Hamiltonian (see Proposition 25 in the Supplementary Information):

\bar{\Delta}\geq\Delta\left(1-\frac{3}{2}\delta^{2}\right),\quad\||\Omega\rangle-|\bar{\Omega}\rangle\|\leq\delta,

(50)

provided that the parameter
\epsilon
is chosen as
\epsilon=\mathcal{O}\left(\delta^{2}\log^{-\mathfrak{a}k/2}(\delta^{-1})\right)
. This truncation partially resolves the issue of unboundedness: while the local energy near the boundary becomes bounded due to the stricter cutoff, it remains unbounded in regions far from the boundary. The remaining unboundedness will be addressed by subsequent truncation procedures.

In the next truncation of Eq. (
48
), we perform an interaction truncation localized near the boundary. If all long-range interactions across the entire system were truncated uniformly, the resulting Hamiltonian
H_{\mathrm{t}}
would differ significantly from the previously truncated Hamiltonian
\bar{H}
, with an operator norm distance
\|\bar{H}-H_{\mathrm{t}}\|=\mathcal{O}(|\Lambda|)
. Such a global modification would substantially alter the ground state and degrade its fidelity. To avoid this, we confine the truncation of long-range interactions to a finite region around the interface between the subsystems
L
and
R
.
Specifically, we partition the neighborhood near the boundary into
q
blocks
\{B_{s}\}_{s=1}^{q}
, each of length
l
, and define the remaining outer regions as
B_{0}
and
B_{q+1}
on the left and right, respectively. The interaction truncation is implemented such that only adjacent blocks are allowed to interact, and all longer-range interactions across non-neighboring blocks are eliminated. The resulting truncated Hamiltonian is given by

H_{\mathrm{t}}=\sum_{s=0}^{q+1}h_{s}+\sum_{s=0}^{q}h_{s,s+1},

(51)

where
h_{s}
denotes the internal Hamiltonian acting within block
B_{s}
, and
h_{s,s+1}
represents the interaction between adjacent blocks
B_{s}
and
B_{s+1}
.
The truncation error, measured by
\|\bar{H}-H_{\mathrm{t}}\|
, can be effectively controlled by choosing the number of blocks
q
and their length
l
appropriately (see Proposition 26 in the Supplementary Information):

\|\bar{H}-H_{\mathrm{t}}\|\leq\mathcal{O}\left(q\log^{\mathfrak{a}k/2}(ql)\cdot l^{2}\bar{J}(l)\right),

(52)

where
\bar{J}(l)
characterizes the decay of long-range interactions, defined in Eq. (
12
).
Moreover, the spectral gap
\Delta_{\mathrm{t}}
and the ground state
|\Omega_{\mathrm{t}}\rangle
of
H_{\mathrm{t}}
remain close to those of
\bar{H}
, with the following bounds:

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathrm{t}}\geq\bar{\Delta}-2\|\bar{H}-H_{\mathrm{t}}\|,

(53)

\displaystyle\||\bar{\Omega}\rangle-|\Omega_{\mathrm{t}}\rangle\|\leq\frac{\|\bar{H}-H_{\mathrm{t}}\|}{\bar{\Delta}-4\|\bar{H}-H_{\mathrm{t}}\|}.

The third step in Eq. (
48
) involves applying multi-energy truncations within all blocks—not just at the boundaries—in order to control the operator norm of the truncated Hamiltonian. For each intra-block Hamiltonian
\{h_{s}\}_{s=0}^{q+1}
, we truncate its spectrum by projecting onto the subspace of eigenvalues less than or equal to
\tau_{s}:=E_{s,0}+\tau
, where
E_{s,0}
denotes the ground-state energy of
h_{s}
and
\tau
is a tunable cutoff parameter.
Let
\Pi_{\leq\tau_{s}}^{(s)}
be the corresponding spectral projection for block
B_{s}
, and define the total projection as

\tilde{\Pi}:=\bigotimes_{s=0}^{q+1}\Pi_{\leq\tau_{s}}^{(s)}.

(54)

The fully truncated Hamiltonian is then given by
\tilde{H}_{\mathrm{t}}:=\tilde{\Pi}H_{\mathrm{t}}\tilde{\Pi}
.
At this stage, we must also truncate the boundary blocks
B_{0}
and
B_{q+1}
, which still remained unbounded. Ensuring the preservation of the ground state and spectral gap under this truncation is technically the most delicate part of the analysis.

A major difficulty arises from the fact that the unbounded Hamiltonian
H_{\mathrm{t}}
renders the imaginary-time evolution
e^{\beta H_{\mathrm{t}}}Oe^{-\beta H_{\mathrm{t}}}
ill-defined due to divergence.
For bounded local Hamiltonians, robustness of the ground state under truncation is typically proven by controlling imaginary-time evolution, bounding expressions of the form
\left\|e^{\beta H_{\mathrm{t}}}Oe^{-\beta H_{\mathrm{t}}}\right\|
via convergent expansions in multi-commutators
[
51
]
.
This approach crucially relies on the fact that
\left\|\mathrm{ad}_{H_{\mathrm{t}}}^{m}(O)\right\|
grows at most factorially (or more generally, in a way that still yields a finite bound after summing
\sum_{m}\beta^{m}\left\|\mathrm{ad}_{H_{\mathrm{t}}}^{m}(O)\right\|/m!
).
In bosonic systems, however
H_{\mathrm{t}}
is unbounded, and multi-commutators exhibit super-factorial growth of the form
\left\|\mathrm{ad}_{H_{\mathrm{t}}}^{m}(O)\right\|\lesssim(T_{m}m)^{m}
with
T_{m}
increasing in
m
, so the imaginary-time dressing
e^{\beta\mathrm{ad}_{H_{\mathrm{t}}}}(O)
fails to be well controlled and may diverge.
To overcome this difficulty, we follow the alternative strategy of Ref.
[
60
]
, which avoids imaginary-time evolution altogether.
Instead of using
e^{\beta H_{\mathrm{t}}}
, the analysis employs a polynomial energy filter
(E+\theta-H_{\mathrm{t}})^{m}
, where
\theta>0
denotes the energy separation between the low-energy sector
\Pi_{\mathrm{t},\leq E}
and the excited sector under consideration.
Expanding this filter in multi-commutators and optimizing over the degree
m
yields explicit bounds on matrix elements of the form
\left\|\Pi_{\mathrm{t},[E+\theta,E+\theta+T_{0}]}O\Pi_{\mathrm{t},\leq E}\right\|
.
These bounds decay subexponentially in
\theta
.
They are sufficient to show that the multi-energy truncated projector
\tilde{\Pi}
captures almost all of the ground-state weight.
Consequently, the effective Hamiltonian
\tilde{H}_{\mathrm{t}}=\tilde{\Pi}H_{\mathrm{t}}\tilde{\Pi}
preserves the ground state and the spectral gap up to a subexponentially small error in the cutoff parameter
\tau
.

While the preservation of ground-state properties under bounded Hamiltonians is well understood
[
51
]
, the unbounded setting introduces significant analytical challenges.
To overcome this issue, we adopt an alternative approach developed in Ref.
[
60
]
.

This leads to a modified robustness theorem for the ground state and spectral gap of
\tilde{H}_{\mathrm{t}}
(see Theorem 4 in the Supplementary Information):

\tilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{t}}\geq\Delta_{\mathrm{t}}-3\epsilon^{\prime},\quad\||\Omega_{\mathrm{t}}\rangle-|\tilde{\Omega}_{\mathrm{t}}\rangle\|\leq\sqrt{\frac{2\epsilon^{\prime}}{\Delta_{\mathrm{t}}}},

(55)

where

\epsilon^{\prime}=\mathcal{O}\left(\exp\left[-q\left(\tau+\log^{\mathfrak{a}k/2}(ql)\right)^{1/(1+\mathfrak{a}k/2)}\right]\right).

(56)

We point out that Ref.
[
48
]
directly applied the robustness theorem from Ref.
[
12
]
—originally proved for bounded Hamiltonians—to the case of unbounded bosonic systems. However, such an application does not directly extend to the unbounded setting: the key technical assumptions and estimates used in the bounded case do not necessarily hold for unbounded Hamiltonians, which require different analytical tools (see Supplementary Note 10.B for a detailed discussion).
This is because the proof techniques for bounded Hamiltonians rely crucially on norm estimates and operator inequalities that can break down in the unbounded setting, necessitating a different analytical approach.
In our work, we resolve this issue by employing a framework tailored to unbounded Hamiltonians
[
60
]
, which allows us to rigorously control the ground state and spectral gap under energy truncation in our setup. As a consequence, the truncation error
\epsilon^{\prime}
in our setting exhibits subexponential decay in the energy cutoff
\tau
, in contrast to the exponential decay observed in bounded cases. This difference reflects the essential analytical distinction between bounded spin models and unbounded bosonic models and underscores the necessity of a refined treatment in the present setting.

Finally, with the effective Hamiltonian
\tilde{H}_{\mathrm{t}}
at hand, we follow the same strategy as in Refs.
[
12
,
29
]
to establish the area law by constructing an AGSP based on Chebyshev polynomials. The approximation error of the AGSP scales as
e^{-m\sqrt{\Delta/\|\tilde{H}_{\mathrm{t}}\|}}
(see Lemma 27 in the Supplementary Information), where
m
is the degree of the polynomial and
\Delta
denotes the spectral gap. The corresponding Schmidt rank is also shown to remain within acceptable bounds (see Lemma 28). By optimizing all relevant parameters—including the boson-number cutoff profile, the number and size of the blocks, and the polynomial degree—we construct an AGSP that satisfies the desired approximation properties (see Lemma 24 and Proposition 29). Iterating this AGSP yields an upper bound on the entanglement entropy (Lemma 33) and establishes a quantitative bound on the Schmidt rank required to approximate the ground state (Proposition 30), thereby completing the proof of the entanglement area law for one-dimensional bosonic systems with long-range interactions.

The data that support the findings of this study are available within the paper and its Supplementary Information.

D.K. and T.K. contributed to the conceptualization, methodology, data analysis, and drafting of the manuscript.

Supplementary Note 1
Set up and general notations

Supplementary Note 1.1
Setup

Consider a quantum system on a
D
-dimensional lattice with
n
sites, with
\Lambda
representing the set of all sites.
For any arbitrary partial set
X\subseteq\Lambda
, we denote the cardinality (number of sites in
X
) as
|X|
. The complementary subset of
X
is denoted by
X^{\rm c}:=\Lambda\setminus X
.
For subsets
X
and
Y
of
\Lambda
, the distance
d_{X,Y}
is defined as the shortest path length on the graph connecting
X
and
Y
, with
d_{X,Y}=0
if
X\cap Y\neq\emptyset
. When
X
comprises only one element (i.e.,
X=\{i\}
), we use
d_{i,Y}
to represent
d_{\{i\},Y}
for simplicity.
We also define
{\rm diam}(X)
as follows:

\displaystyle{\rm diam}(X):=1+\max_{i,i^{\prime}\in X}(d_{i,i^{\prime}}).

(S.1)

The surface subset of
X
is denoted by

\displaystyle\partial X:=\{i\in X\,|\,d_{i,X^{\rm c}}=1\}.

(S.2)

For a subset
X\subseteq\Lambda
, the extended subset
X[r]
is defined as

\displaystyle X[r]:=\{i\in\Lambda\,|\,d_{X,i}\leq r\},

(S.3)

where
X[0]=X
, and
r
is an arbitrary positive number (i.e.,
r\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
). We introduce a geometric parameter
\gamma
determined solely by the lattice structure, satisfying
\gamma=\mathcal{O}(1)
, which fulfills the inequalities:

\displaystyle\max_{i\in\Lambda}\left(|\partial i[r]|\right)\leq\gamma(r^{D-1}+1),\quad\max_{i\in\Lambda}|i[r]|\leq\gamma(r^{D}+1),

(S.4)

where
r\geq 1
.

Supplementary Note 1.2
Boson operators

We define
b_{i}
and
b_{i}^{\dagger}
as the annihilation and the creation operators of a boson, respectively.
We also define
\hat{n}_{i}
as the number operator of bosons on a site
i\in\Lambda
, namely
\hat{n}_{i}\coloneqq b_{i}^{\dagger}b_{i}
.
For an arbitrary set
\mathbf{N}=\{N_{i}\}_{i\in\Lambda}
, we define the Mott state
|\mathbf{N}\rangle
as such

\displaystyle\hat{n}_{i}|\mathbf{N}\rangle=N_{i}|\mathbf{N}\rangle\quad\textrm{for}\quad\forall i\in\Lambda.

(S.5)

We adopt the notation of
\Pi_{i,N}
(
i\subseteq\Lambda
) as the projection onto the eigenspace of
\hat{n}_{i}
with the eigenvalue
N
:

\displaystyle\hat{n}_{i}\Pi_{i,N}=N\Pi_{i,N}.

(S.6)

We also define
\Pi_{i,\geq N}
(
\Pi_{i,<N}
) as the projection operator onto the space such that the boson number at the site
i
is larger than (smaller than)
N
:

\displaystyle\Pi_{i,\geq N}=\sum_{N_{1}\geq N}\Pi_{i,N_{1}},\quad\Pi_{i,<N}=\sum_{N_{1}<N}\Pi_{i,N_{1}}.

(S.7)

Here, for an arbitrary subset
X\subseteq\Lambda
, we denote
\Pi_{X,\geq N}
(
\Pi_{X,<N}
) as

\displaystyle\Pi_{X,\geq N}=\bigotimes_{i\in X}\Pi_{i,\geq N},\quad\Pi_{X,<N}=\bigotimes_{i\in X}\Pi_{i,<N}.

(S.8)

Supplementary Note 1.3
General bosonic Hamiltonians

We often decompose the Hamiltonian in the form of

\displaystyle H=\sum_{Z:Z\subset\Lambda}h_{Z},

(S.9)

where
h_{Z}
composed of the boson operators
\{b_{i}\}_{i\in Z}
and
\{b^{\dagger}_{i}\}_{i\in Z}
.
We adopt the notation of the subset Hamiltonian
H_{X}
as follows:

\displaystyle H_{X}=\sum_{Z:Z\subseteq X}h_{Z}.

(S.10)

We also denote
\widehat{H_{X}}
by

\displaystyle\widehat{H_{X}}=\sum_{Z:Z\cap X\neq\emptyset}h_{Z},

(S.11)

which gives

\displaystyle H=\widehat{H_{X}}+H_{X^{\rm c}}.

(S.12)

We introduce the parameters
g
and
k
as follows:

Definition 1

(Parameters
g
and
k
)
.

Under the decomposition of Eq. (
S.9
), we define
g
and
k
by the constants that do not depend on the system size
|\Lambda|
and satisfy

\displaystyle\max_{i:i\in\Lambda}\sum_{Z:Z\ni i}\left\|h_{Z}\Pi_{\Lambda,\leq N}\right\|\leq gN^{k/2},

(S.13)

where
g
is an
\mathcal{O}(1)
constant.

Also, we define the ground state
|\Omega\rangle
and the spectral gap
\Delta
as follows:

Definition 2

(Ground state)
.

We define the state
|\Omega\rangle
as the minimum energy state:

\displaystyle\langle\Omega|H|\Omega\rangle\leq\langle\psi|H|\psi\rangle

(S.14)

for an arbitrary quantum state
|\psi\rangle
.
We define the spectral gap
\Delta
as the energy difference between the ground state and the first excited state:

\displaystyle\Delta\leq\langle\psi_{\bot}|H|\psi_{\bot}\rangle-\langle\Omega|H|\Omega\rangle

(S.15)

for an arbitrary quantum state
|\psi_{\bot}\rangle
such that
\langle\Omega|\psi_{\bot}\rangle=0
.
In particular, if the ground state is degenerate, we let
\Delta=0
.

Part 1
Boson number distribution

Supplementary Note 2
Bose-Hubbard classes and
\phi 4
class

Supplementary Note 2.1
Assumption for the Bose-Hubbard class

To treat the bosonic Hamiltonian, we have to restrict the class of the Hamiltonian
H
in Eq. (
S.9
).
We consider the most general form of the interacting boson systems up to
k
th degree:

\displaystyle H={\mathcal{F}}_{H}(\mathbf{b},\mathbf{b}^{\dagger}),

(S.16)

where
{\mathcal{F}}_{H}(\mathbf{b},\mathbf{b}^{\dagger})
is an arbitrary
k
-degree polynomial of
\mathbf{b}=\{b_{i}\}_{i\in\Lambda}
and
\mathbf{b}^{\dagger}=\{b^{\dagger}_{i}\}_{i\in\Lambda}
.
We decompose the term that commutes with the boson number operator
\{\hat{n}_{i}\}_{i\in\Lambda}
and rewrite the above form as

\displaystyle H=H_{0}(\mathbf{b},\mathbf{b}^{\dagger})+V_{+}(\mathbf{n})+\sum_{i\in\Lambda}U_{i}\hat{n}_{i}^{k/2},

(S.17)

where
U_{i}>0
for
\forall i\in\Lambda
,
H_{0}(\mathbf{b},\mathbf{b}^{\dagger})
is a
k
-degree polynomial of
\mathbf{b}=\{b_{i}\}_{i\in\Lambda}
and
\mathbf{b}^{\dagger}=\{b^{\dagger}_{i}\}_{i\in\Lambda}
, and
V_{+}(\mathbf{n})
is a positive
(k/2)
-degree polynomial function with respect to
\mathbf{n}=\{\hat{n}_{i}\}_{i\in\Lambda}
.
Here, the Hamiltonian
H_{0}(\mathbf{b},\mathbf{b}^{\dagger})
consists of operators that have negative eigenvalues (e.g.,
-n_{i}n_{j}
,
b_{i}b_{j}^{\dagger}+{\rm h.c.}
,
n_{i}b_{j}b_{k}+{\rm h.c.}
and so on).
To consider the non-trivial boson-boson interactions, we treat the case of
k\geq 3
.
The case of
k=2
is the so-called bilinear Hamiltonian, and its properties, including the entanglement area law, have been extensively investigated
[
46
]
.

Most of the interesting boson models are reduced to Eq. (
S.17
) by appropriately choosing the degree
k
*1
*1

*1

Typically, it is enough to consider
k=4
.
.
For example, by letting
k=4
and choosing in Eq. (
S.17
) as

\displaystyle H_{0}(\mathbf{b},\mathbf{b}^{\dagger})\to\sum_{\left\langle i,j\right\rangle}J_{i,j}\left(b_{i}b_{j}^{\dagger}+{\rm h.c.}\right)-U\sum_{i\in\Lambda}\hat{n}_{i},\quad V_{+}(\mathbf{n})\to 0,\quad U_{i}\to U,

(S.18)

we obtain the Bose-Hubbard model.

Remark.
In the previous studies
[
36
,
48
]
, boson models such that
H_{0}(\mathbf{b},\mathbf{b}^{\dagger})
is linear function with respect to
\mathbf{b},\mathbf{b}^{\dagger}
are considered [see Ineq. (6a), (6b) and (6c) in Ref.
[
36
]
]
*2
*2

*2

More precisely, the interaction between bosons and fermions, e.g.,
b_{i}(c_{i}^{\dagger}c_{i})+{\rm h.c.}
with
c_{i},c_{i}^{\dagger}
the fermion operators at the site
i\in\Lambda
, can be included.
.
Hence, we cannot apply the method to a standard boson hopping operator like
b_{i}b_{j}^{\dagger}
and the
\phi^{4}
terms in Eq. (
S.28
), which has been thought to be a significant and challenging open question.

We need to exclude the possibility that the ground state is not well-defined in the thermodynamic limit, where an infinite number of bosons may accumulate on a single site.
It indeed happens in the standard Bose-Hubbard model (
S.18
) when the on-site boson-boson interaction is attractive.
We, therefore, need an assumption to exclude the possibility.

In the case where
H_{0}(\mathbf{b},\mathbf{b}^{\dagger})
is given by a polynomial up to
(k-1)
th order, the assumption of
U_{i}>0
(
i\in\Lambda
) in Eq. (
S.17
) is enough (see Assumption
1
below).
However, when the repulsive and the attractive interactions have the same order, the condition becomes highly non-trivial.
As a convenient notation, we first adopt the following definition:

Definition 3
.

Let us pick up the
k_{1}
th order terms in
H_{0}(\mathbf{b},\mathbf{b}^{\dagger})
and denote them by

\displaystyle H_{0}^{(k_{1})}(\mathbf{b},\mathbf{b}^{\dagger})=\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{k_{1}}\in\Lambda}\sum_{s=1}^{k_{1}+1}J^{(s)}_{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{k_{1}}}b_{i_{k_{1}}}^{\dagger}\cdots b_{i_{s+1}}^{\dagger}b_{i_{s}}^{\dagger}b_{i_{s-1}}\cdots b_{i_{2}}b_{i_{1}},

(S.19)

where the site indices
i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{k_{1}}
can be identical to each other
*3
*3

*3

We note that any interaction forms can be expressed in the form of Eq. (
S.19
).
.
We then define the parameters
\bar{J}_{i,k_{1}}
and
\bar{J}_{k_{1}}
as

\displaystyle\bar{J}_{i,k_{1}}:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{k_{1}}\in\Lambda\\
\{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{k_{1}}\}\ni i\end{subarray}}\sum_{s=1}^{k_{1}}\left|J^{(s)}_{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{k_{1}}}\right|,

\displaystyle\bar{J}_{k_{1}}:=\max_{i\in\Lambda}(\bar{J}_{i,k_{1}}).

(S.20)

We note if
H_{0}(\mathbf{b},\mathbf{b}^{\dagger})
includes up to
(k_{1}-1)
-degree, we have
\bar{J}_{i,k_{1}}=0
.

Using the parameter
\bar{J}_{i}
, we write the assumption as follows:

Assumption 1

(Repulsive condition)
.

In the Hamiltonian (
S.17
), we call the interaction repulsive if

\displaystyle U_{i}>5\bar{J}_{i,k}\quad\textrm{for}\quad\forall i\in\Lambda.

(S.21)

Note that the condition reduces to
U_{i}>0
in the case where
H_{0}(\mathbf{b},\mathbf{b}^{\dagger})
is given by a
(k-1)
-degree polynomial.

The specific numerical constant appearing here is chosen for technical convenience; the analysis depends only on the repulsive on-site term being dominant over the interaction terms, and different choices within this regime affect only the numerical bounds, not the qualitative physical conclusions.

Also, for the convenience of our analyses,
we define a similar decomposition for
V_{+}(\mathbf{n})
In a similar way to Def.
3
:

Definition 4
.

The operator
V_{+}(\mathbf{n})
is generally written as

\displaystyle V_{+}(\mathbf{n})=\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{k/2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{k_{1}}\in\Lambda\end{subarray}}V_{+,i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{k_{1}}}\hat{n}_{i_{1}}\hat{n}_{i_{2}}\cdots\hat{n}_{i_{k_{1}}},\quad V_{+,i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{k_{1}}}>0.

(S.22)

We also define the parameter
\bar{v}
as

\displaystyle\bar{v}:=\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{k/2}\bar{v}_{k_{1}},

\displaystyle\bar{v}_{k_{1}}:=\max_{i\in\Lambda}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{k_{1}}\in\Lambda\\
\{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{k_{1}}\}\ni i\end{subarray}}V_{+,i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{k_{1}}}.

(S.23)

Supplementary Note 2.2
Assumption for the
\phi 4
class

In the
\phi 4
-class of the interacting boson systems, we consider the following
\phi
operator and
\pi
operator:

\displaystyle\phi=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(b+b^{\dagger}),\quad\pi=\frac{-i}{\sqrt{2}}(b-b^{\dagger}),

(S.24)

where we omit the site index.
From the bosonic commutator relation as
[b,b^{\dagger}]=1
,
the field operator
\phi
and its conjugate momentum
\pi
satisfy the following canonical commutation relation:

\displaystyle[\phi,\pi]=i.

(S.25)

Using the notation
\phi
and
\pi
, we consider the following form of the Hamiltonian:

\displaystyle H=\sum_{i\in\Lambda}\mu_{i}\pi_{i}^{2}+{\mathcal{F}}(\bm{\phi}),

(S.26)

where
{\mathcal{F}}(\bm{\phi})
is an arbitrary
k
-degree function of
\bm{\phi}=\{\phi_{i}\}_{i\in\Lambda}
.
We denote the upper bound of
\left|\mu_{i}\right|
by
\bar{\mu}
:

\displaystyle\bar{\mu}:=\max_{i\in\Lambda}(\left|\mu_{i}\right|).

(S.27)

The representative case is the
\phi 4
Hamiltonian as follows:

\displaystyle H=\sum_{i\in\Lambda}\left(\pi_{i}^{2}+\phi_{i}^{2}+\lambda\phi_{i}^{4}\right)+\gamma\sum_{\left\langle i,i^{\prime}\right\rangle}\phi_{i}\phi_{i^{\prime}}

(S.28)

By replacing
\phi_{i}
and
\pi_{i}
using Eq. (
S.24
), the Hamiltonian becomes:

\displaystyle H=\sum_{i}\left[2b_{i}^{\dagger}b_{i}+1+\frac{\lambda}{4}(b_{i}+b_{i}^{\dagger})^{4}\right]+\frac{\gamma}{2}\sum_{\langle i,i^{\prime}\rangle}(b_{i}+b_{i}^{\dagger})(b_{i^{\prime}}+b_{i^{\prime}}^{\dagger}).

(S.29)

As a significant problem, we can see that Assumption
1
is not satisfied in the
\phi 4
class.
To see the point, we look at the highest-order term
(b_{i}+b_{i}^{\dagger})^{4}
, whose ground state has infinite boson density.
Hence, we can prove that there exists a product state
|\psi\rangle=\bigotimes_{i\in\Lambda}|\mu_{i}\rangle
such that

\displaystyle\langle\psi|(b_{i}+b_{i}^{\dagger})^{4}|\psi\rangle\approx 0,\quad{\rm but}\quad\langle\psi|\hat{n}_{i}|\psi\rangle\gg 1.

(S.30)

This means that the operator
(b_{i}+b_{i}^{\dagger})^{4}
does not satisfy the repulsive-interaction condition as in Assumption
1
.

We need a qualitatively different approach to the
\phi 4
class.
Significantly, we only need the following simple condition for the Hamiltonian class as in Eq. (
S.26
).

Assumption 2

(Parity symmetry)
.

We assume the Hamiltonian is invariant under
\bm{\phi}\to-\bm{\phi}
. This is satisfied when
{\mathcal{F}}(\bm{\phi})
is given by an even function as follows:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}(\bm{\phi})=\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{k/2}\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{2k_{1}}\in\Lambda}f_{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{2k_{1}}}\phi_{i_{1}}\phi_{i_{2}}\cdots\phi_{i_{2k_{1}}},

(S.31)

where
k
is an even integer, and
i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{2k_{1}}
can be identical to each other.
